(8.12)

Using (see e.g. equation (
4.16
))

(Q^{\Lambda_{1}}_{\beta\alpha}\otimes Q^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}})Q^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}}=\sum_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}D^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(Q^{\Lambda_{1}}_{\beta\alpha}\otimes Q^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}})Q^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}},

(8.13)

we have

\displaystyle\Tr_{V_{N}^{\otimes k}}((Q^{\Lambda_{1}}_{\beta\alpha}\otimes Q^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}})Q^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}D^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(Q^{\Lambda_{1}}_{\beta\alpha}\otimes Q^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}})\Tr_{V_{N}^{\otimes k}}(Q^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}})

(8.14)

\displaystyle=D^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(Q^{\Lambda_{1}}_{\beta\alpha}\otimes Q^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}})\Dim V^{S_{N}}_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Dim V^{S_{N}}_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}\vbox{\hbox{

The second equality uses (
A.65
).

To further simplify, we want to turn the RHS into a product of matrix elements. This is achieved by inserting a resolution of the identity using representations of
P_{k_{1}}(N)\otimes P_{k_{2}}(N)
.
This resolves to a set of branching coefficients for
P_{k}(N)\rightarrow P_{k_{1}}(N)\otimes P_{k_{2}}(N)
. We denote these by

B^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}\otimes\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime},\xi}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}

(8.15)

where it is implicit that
k=k_{1}+k_{2}
. The ranges of the labels are

\displaystyle\gamma\in[1,\dots,\Dim\big(V_{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}}^{P_{k_{1}}(N)}\big)],

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}\in[1,\dots,\Dim\big(V_{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime}}^{P_{k_{2}}(N)}\big)],

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime\prime}\in[1,\dots,\Dim\big(V_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{P_{k}(N)}\big)],

\displaystyle\xi\in[1,\dots,\Mult\big(V_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{P_{k}(N)}\rightarrow V_{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}}^{P_{k_{1}}(N)}\otimes V_{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime}}^{P_{k_{2}}(N)}\big)],

(8.16)

the final label,
\xi
, gives the multiplicity of
\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}
in the decomposition.

(8.17)

It is worth noting that by Schur-Weyl duality the branching multiplicities for partition algebras are related to the multiplicities
C(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1},\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime},\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime})
, known as Kronecker coefficients, of irreducible
representations
\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}
in tensor products of
S_{N}
representations
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}\otimes\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime}
(see eq. (3.1.3) of
[
119
]
)

\Mult\Big(V_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{P_{k}(N)}\rightarrow V_{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}}^{P_{k_{1}}(N)}\otimes V_{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime}}^{P_{k_{2}}(N)}\Big)=C(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1},\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime},\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}).

(8.18)

For simiplicity we are assuming
N\geq(2k_{1}+2k_{2})
. For comparison, in Schur-Weyl duality between
U(N)
and
\mathbb{C}[S_{k}]
, Littlewood-Richardson coefficients are branching multiplicities for
S_{k_{1}+k_{2}}\rightarrow S_{k_{1}}\times S_{k_{2}}
but correspond to decomposition of tensor products of
U(N)
representations.

Branching coefficients are equivariant:

D^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\delta^{\prime\prime}}(d_{\pi_{1}}\otimes d_{\pi_{2}})=\sum_{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1},\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime},\gamma,\delta,\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime},\xi}B^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}\otimes\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime},\xi}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}D^{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}}_{\gamma\delta}(d_{\pi_{1}})D^{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime}}_{\gamma^{\prime}\delta^{\prime}}(d_{\pi_{2}})B^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}\otimes\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime},\xi}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\delta\delta^{\prime}},

(8.19)

for
d_{\pi_{1}}\in P_{k_{1}}(N),d_{\pi_{2}}\in P_{k_{2}}(N)
.
Setting
d_{\pi_{1}}=Q^{\Lambda_{1}}_{\alpha\beta},d_{\pi_{2}}=Q^{{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}}_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}
, equation (
8.19
) corresponds to the diagram identity

(8.20)

Inserting this into equation (
8.14
) gives

\displaystyle\Tr_{V_{N}^{\otimes k}}(

\displaystyle(Q^{\Lambda_{1}}_{\beta\alpha}\otimes Q^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}})~Q^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}})=

\displaystyle\Dim V^{S_{N}}_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}\sum_{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1},\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime},\gamma,\eta,\gamma^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},\xi}B^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}\otimes\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime},\xi}_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}D^{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}}_{\gamma\eta}(Q^{\Lambda_{1}}_{\alpha\beta})D^{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime}}_{\gamma^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}(Q^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}})B^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}\otimes\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime},\xi}_{\eta^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\eta\eta^{\prime}}.

(8.21)

Matrix elements of irreducible representations are orthogonal (see equation (
A.42
)). This implies

D^{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\eta^{\prime\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}}(Q^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}})=\delta^{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}\delta_{\eta^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}}\delta_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}}

(8.24)

This gives the final result for matrix units of
P_{k}(N)
.

The full expression for (
8.11
) - extremal three-point correlators in the representation basis - is given by (
8.24
) together with branching coefficients from the partition algebras to symmetric group algebras (see (
3.53
)),

\displaystyle\Tr_{V_{N}^{\otimes k}}

\displaystyle\Big(\big(Q^{\Lambda_{1}}_{\Lambda_{2},\nu\mu}\otimes Q^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{2}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}\big)Q^{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}_{\Lambda_{2}^{\prime\prime},\mu^{\prime\prime}\nu^{\prime\prime}}\Big)=\Dim V_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{S_{N}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\beta,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime},\\
p,p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime},\xi\end{subarray}}B^{{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow{\Lambda}_{1}\otimes{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime},\xi}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}B^{{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow{\Lambda}_{1}\otimes{\Lambda}_{1}^{\prime},\xi}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\beta\beta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle B^{P_{k_{1}}(N)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[S_{k_{1}}]}_{\Lambda_{1},\alpha\rightarrow\Lambda_{2},p;\mu}B^{P_{k_{1}}(N)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[S_{k_{1}}]}_{\Lambda_{1},\beta\rightarrow\Lambda_{2},p;\nu}B^{P_{k_{2}}(N)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[S_{k_{2}}]}_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\rightarrow\Lambda_{2}^{\prime},p^{\prime};\mu^{\prime}}B^{P_{k_{2}}(N)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[S_{k_{2}}]}_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\rightarrow\Lambda_{2}^{\prime},p^{\prime};\nu^{\prime}}B^{P_{k}(N)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[S_{k}]}_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\Lambda_{2}^{\prime\prime},p^{\prime\prime};\mu^{\prime\prime}}B^{P_{k}(N)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[S_{k}]}_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\Lambda_{2}^{\prime\prime},p^{\prime\prime};\nu^{\prime\prime}}.

(8.27)

From the above formula we see that the extremal correlator vanishes if the Kronecker coefficient
C(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{1}^{\prime},\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime})=0
. Analogous results for extremal correlators in general quiver gauge theories are described in
[
64
]
.

9
Summary and Outlook

In this paper we investigated the effects of permutation symmetry on the state space and dynamics of quantum mechanical systems of
N\times N
matrix variables.
After a brief review of the matrix harmonic oscillator and introduction of notation in section
2
, we began in section
3
by investigating the
S_{N}
invariant Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\text{inv}}
of generic matrix quantum mechanics systems at large
N
. We found that there is a one-to-one correspondence between
S_{N}
invariant states of degree
k
and elements in the symmetrised partition algebra
SP_{k}(N)
. Two bases of
SP_{k}(N)
were discussed: the diagram basis and the representation basis. A construction of the latter was explained in section
4
in terms of diagonalizing commuting algebraic charges. Having discussed the
S_{N}
invariant state space, we moved on to interesting invariant Hamiltonians. The general permutation invariant harmonic matrix oscillator was described and solved (diagonalized) in section
5
. This was achieved with the introduction of oscillators labelled by representation theoretic quantities, as in (
5.27
). In section
6
we described a set of algebraic Hamiltonians for matrix quantum mechanics that preserve the
S_{N}
invariant subspace of the Hilbert space. These Hamiltonians, given by equations (
6.38
), (
6.3
) and (
6.44
) realise the three dynamical scenarios illustrated on the left hand side of figure
1(a)
, the right hand side of figure
1(a)
, and figure
1(b)
respectively. The representation basis introduced in section
3.3
diagonalizes all of these algebraic Hamiltonians. We provided a lattice interpretation of the matrix oscillators in section
6.6
. In section
7
we constructed Hamiltonians which turn the
S_{N}
invariant state space into quantum many-body scars. Following the ideas in
[
85
,
86
]
, we gave Hamiltonians (
7.2
) of the form
H+H_{s}
where
H
is
S_{N}
invariant and
H_{s}
annihilates states in the
S_{N}
invariant subspace. We noted that the Hamiltonians in section
6
are suitable candidates for
H
if their energies satisfy an integrality condition. As an example, we used the lattice interpretation to give a modified Bose-Hubbard Hamiltonian which exhibits
S_{N}
invariant quantum many-body scars.
The diagram basis is the most efficient basis for describing inner and outer products. As a consequence extremal correlators, defined in (
8.6
), which are analogues of three-point extremal correlators in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM are simple in the diagram basis. The extremal correlators satisfy representation theoretic selection rules, based on Kronecker coefficients, which were derived in the representation basis. The selection rules are based on exact expressions for extremal correlators, involving Kronecker coefficients and Littlewood-Richardson coefficients, given in equation (
8.2
).

The representation theoretic basis
Q^{\Lambda_{1}}_{\Lambda_{2},\mu\nu}
for the

S_{N}
invariant Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\text{inv}}
constructed as linear
combinations of symmetrised partition algebra elements in
SP_{k}(N)

in section
3.3
is an eigenstate basis for the free Hamiltonian
H_{0}
of section
2
as well as the algebraic Hamiltonians constructed in section

6
. The action of the general permutation invariant harmonic oscillator Hamiltonian given in (
5.27
) of section
5
however causes a non-trivial mixing of the representation labels. This mixing was discussed briefly in section
5.4
. Diagonalising the general harmonic oscillator Hamiltonians in
\mathcal{H}_{\text{inv}}
is an interesting, unsolved problem of finding appropriate linear combinations of the
Q^{\Lambda_{1}}_{\Lambda_{2},\mu\nu}
which are invariant, up to scaling, under the mixing.

With the exception of the
P_{k}(N)
orbit basis discussion in appendix
B
we have assumed
N\geq 2k
, known as the stable limit. This simplified the construction of a basis for the
S_{N}
invariant subspace
\mathcal{H}_{\text{inv}}
, a simplification related to the existence of a kernel free map from
P_{k}(N)
to
\text{End}(V_{N}^{\otimes k})
. However, it would be interesting to uncover any finite
N
effects appearing in these permutation invariant quantum mechanical matrix systems.
At finite
N
the diagrams in
P_{k}(N)
provide an over complete basis of operators. That is, there are some linear relations between operators. The precise form of these relations can be found using the orbit basis. The question remains of how to use this knowledge in order to construct a representation theoretic basis for
2k<N
. We leave this for future work, but note here that it would involve a detailed study of the Artin-Wedderburn decomposition in (
3.48
) below the stable limit. The detailed study includes putting constraints on the irreducible representations appearing in the decomposition below the stable limit, as well as computing the dimension of the multiplicity spaces.

In section
6.6
we gave one interpretation of our model in terms of bosonic excitations
(a^{\dagger})_{i}^{j}
on an
N
-by-
N
lattice with sites labelled by
(i,j)
. It is natural to ask if the
S_{N}
invariant Hamiltonians described by (
6.44
) interpreted in this way can be realised in experiments. In the real world interactions tend to be local. The demand for these Hamiltonians to be local places restrictions on the sets of permissible terms. In section
7.3
we used this interpretation to construct a modified Bose-Hubbard Hamiltonian exhibiting
S_{N}
invariant quantum many-body scars. More generally, combining the lattice interpretation of matrix oscillators with the group theoretic scheme given in
[
85
,
86
]
, as was done in
7.1
and
7.2
, provides a useful framework for describing systems with many-body scars in
2+1
dimensions.

A very interesting avenue towards applications of the Hilbert spaces and Hamiltonians considered here is to find systems where the permutation invariant sectors described using partition algebras are naturally selected by the physics. For example, in
a Bose-Einstein condensate composed of
N
identical bosons, excited by vibrational modes between pairs of particles, oscillators
(a^{\dagger})^{j}_{i}
exciting the pair
(i,j)
of particles with
i,j\in\{1,\cdots,N\}
would naturally be subject to the kind of
S_{N}
invariance we have considered here. This would provide links between the theoretical application of partition algebras as considered here with the phenomenological modelling of Bose-Einstein physics, e.g. along the lines of
[
120
]
.

As a closing remark, we note that much of the initial study of the representation theory of partition algebras
P_{k}(N)
was done with physical motivations coming from classical statistical models (Potts models) where
k
is the number of lattice sites and
N
is the number of classical states for each lattice site. The transfer matrix of the classical statistical model plays a crucial role in these studies
[
72
,
74
,
73
]
. The present application of partition algebras looks substantially different: we have quantum mechanical matrix oscillators, with matrix size
N
possible values and
k
specifies the sector of quantum states with
k
oscillators acting on the vacuum. Exploring potential dualities relating systems of the kind studied earlier and the matrix quantum systems defined here is a fascinating future direction.
